. For the causal setting, we use the masked attention
\mathbb{S}_{k}(\cdot)
which calculates the softmax probabilities over the first
k
entries of its input and sets the remaining
T-k
entries to zero. This way, the
k
’th prediction of the transformer only utilizes tokens from
1
to
k
and not the future tokens.

Let
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{ik})_{ik=(1,1)}^{(n,k)}
be tokens to be selected by attention (e.g. locally-optimal indices, see Def.
4
). Then, the sequential generalization of (
Att-SVM
) corresponding to
{\cal{L}}^{\texttt{seq}}(\bm{W})
is given by

\min_{\bm{W}}\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}\quad\text{subj. to}\quad(\bm{x}_{i\alpha_{ik}}-\bm{x}_{it})^{\top}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{ik}\geq 1\quad\textnormal{for all}\quad{t\neq\alpha_{ik}},~~k\in[T],~~i\in[n]~~.

(12)

We refer the reader to Appendix
E
which rigorously establishes all RP results for this sequential classification setting. On the other hand, for the causal inference setting SVM should reflect the fact that the model is not allowed to make use of future tokens. Note that the SVM constraints directly arise from softmax calculations. Thus, since attention is masked over the indices
t\leq k
and
k\in[T]
, the SVM constraints should apply over the same mask. Thus, we can consider the straightforward generalization of global-optimality where
\texttt{opt}_{ik}
is the token index with highest score over indices
t\leq k
and introduce an analogous definition for local-optimality. This leads to the following variation of (
12
), which aims to select indices
\alpha_{ik}\in[k]
from the first
k
tokens

\min_{\bm{W}}\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}\quad\text{subj. to}\quad(\bm{x}_{i\alpha_{ik}}-\bm{x}_{it})^{\top}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{ik}\geq 1\quad\textnormal{for all}\quad t\neq\alpha_{ik},~~t\leq k,~~k\in[T],~~i\in[n].

Causal attention is a special case of a general attention mask which can restrict softmax to arbitrary subset of the tokens. Such general masking can be handled similar to (
7
) by enforcing SVM constraints over the nonzero support of the mask. Finally, the discussion so far extends our main theoretical results and focuses on selecting single token per sequence. It can further be enriched by the generalized SVM equivalence developed in Section
6
to select and compose multiple tokens by generalizing (
Gen-SVM
).

8
Related work

8.1
Implicit regularization, matrix factorization, and sparsity

Extensive research has delved into gradient descent’s implicit bias in separable classification tasks, often using logistic or exponentially-tailed losses for margin maximization
[
SHN+18
,
GLSS18
,
NLG+19
,
JT21
,
KPOT21
,
MWG+20
,
JT20
]
. The findings have also been extended to non-separable data using gradient-based techniques
[
JT18
,
JT19
,
JDST20
]
. Implicit bias in regression problems and losses has been investigated, utilizing methods like mirror descent
[
WGL+20
,
GLSS18
,
YKM20
,
VKR19
,
AW20a
,
AW20b
,
ALH21
,
SATA22
]
. Stochastic gradient descent has also been a subject of interest regarding its implicit bias
[
LWM19
,
BGVV20
,
LR20
,
HWLM20
,
LWA22
,
DML21
,
ZWB+21
]
. This extends to the implicit bias of adaptive and momentum-based methods
[
QQ19
,
WMZ+21
,
WMCL21
,
JST21
]
.

In linear classification, GD iterations on logistic loss and separable datasets converge to the hard margin SVM solution
[
SHN+18
,
RZH03
,
ZY05
]
. The attention layer’s softmax nonlinearity behaves similarly, potentially favoring margin-maximizing solutions. Yet, the layer operates on tokens in input sequences, not for direct classification. Its bias leans toward an (
Att-SVM
), selecting relevant tokens while suppressing others. However, formalizing this intuition presents significant challenges: Firstly, our problem is nonconvex (even in terms of the
\bm{W}
-parameterization), introducing new challenges and complexities. Secondly, it requires the introduction of novel concepts such as locally-optimal tokens, demanding a tailored analysis focused on the cones surrounding them. Our findings on the implicit bias of
({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})
-parameterization share conceptual similarities with
[
SRJ04
]
, which proposes and analyzes a max-margin matrix factorization problem. Similar problems have also been studied more recently in the context of neural-collapse phenomena
[
PHD20
]
through an analysis of the implicit bias and regularization path of the unconstrained features model with cross-entropy loss
[
TKVB22
]
. However, a fundamental distinction from these works lies in the fact that attention solves a different max-margin problem that separate tokens. Moreover, our results on
({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})
-parameterization are inherently connected to the rich literature on low-rank factorization
[
GWB+17
,
ACHL19
,
TVS23
,
TBS+16
,
SS21
]
, stimulating further research.
[
TLZO23
]
is the first work to establish the connection between attention and SVM, which is closest to our work. Here, we augment their framework, initially developed for a simpler attention model, to transformers by providing the first guarantees for self/cross-attention layers, nonlinear prediction heads, and realistic global convergence guarantees. While our Assumption
B.2
and local-convergence analysis align with
[
TLZO23
]
, our contributions in global convergence analysis, benefits of overparameterization, and the generalized SVM-equivalence in Section
6
are unique to this work.

It is well-known that attention map (i.e. softmax outputs) act as a feature selection mechanism and reveal the tokens that are relevant to classification. On the other hand, sparsity and lasso regression (i.e.
\ell_{1}
penalization)
[
Don06
,
Tib96
,
TG07
,
CDS01
,
CRT06
]
have been pivotal tools in the statistics literature for feature selection. Softmax and lasso regression exhibit interesting parallels: The Softmax output
\bm{s}=\mathbb{S}({\bm{X}}\bm{W}{\bm{z}})
obeys
\|\bm{s}\|_{\ell_{1}}=1
by design. Softmax is also highly receptive to being sparse because decreasing the temperature (i.e. scaling up the weights
\bm{W}
) eventually leads to a one-hot vector unless all logits are equal. We (also,
[
TLZO23
]
) have used these intuitions to formalize attention as a
token selection mechanism
. This aspect is clearly visible in our primary SVM formulation (
Att-SVM
) which selects precisely one token from each input sequence (i.e. hard attention). Section
6
has also demonstrated how (
Gen-SVM
) can explain more general sparsity patterns by precisely selecting desired tokens and suppressing others. We hope that this SVM-based token-selection viewpoint will motivate future work and deeper connections to the broader feature-selection and compressed sensing literature.

8.2
Attention mechanism and transformers

Transformers, as highlighted by
[
VSP+17
]
, revolutionized the domains of NLP and machine translation. Prior work on self-attention
[
CDL16
,
PTDU16
,
PXS18
,
LFS+17
]
laid the foundation for this transformative paradigm. In contrast to conventional models like MLPs and CNNs, self-attention models employ global interactions to capture feature representations, resulting in exceptional empirical performance.

Despite their achievements, the mechanisms and learning processes of attention layers remain enigmatic. Recent investigations
[
EGKZ22
,
SEO+22
,
ENM22
,
BV22
,
DCL21
]
have concentrated on specific aspects such as sparse function representation, convex relaxations, and expressive power. Expressivity discussions concerning hard-attention
[
Hah20
]
or attention-only architectures
[
DCL21
]
are connected to our findings when
h(\cdot)
is linear. In fact, our work reveals how linear
h
results in attention’s optimization dynamics to collapse on a single token whereas nonlinear
h
provably requires attention to select and compose multiple tokens. This supports the benefits of the MLP layer for expressivity of transformers. There is also a growing body of research aimed at a theoretical comprehension of in-context learning and the role played by the attention mechanism
[
ASA+22
,
LIPO23
,
ACDS23
,
ZFB23
,
BCW+23
,
GRS+23
]
.
[
SEO+22
]
investigate self-attention with linear activation instead of softmax, while
[
ENM22
]
approximate softmax using a linear operation with unit simplex constraints. Their primary goal is to derive convex reformulations for training problems grounded in empirical risk minimization (ERM). In contrast, our methodologies, detailed in equations (
W-ERM
) and (
KQ-ERM
), delve into the nonconvex domain.

[
MRG+20
,
BALA+23
]
offer insights into the implicit bias of optimizing transformers. Specifically,
[
MRG+20
]
provide empirical evidence that an increase in attention weights results in a sparser softmax, which aligns with our theoretical framework.
[
BALA+23
]
study incremental learning and furnish both theory and numerical evidence that increments of the softmax attention weights (
{\bm{K}}{\bm{Q}}^{\top}
) are low-rank. Our theory aligns with this concept, as the SVM formulation (
KQ-SVM
) of
({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})
parameterization inherently exhibits low-rank properties through the nuclear norm objective, rank-
m
constraint, and implicit constraint induced by Lemma
1
.

Several recent works
[
JSL22
,
LWLC23
,
TWCD23
,
NLL+23
,
ORST23
,
NNH+23
,
FGBM23
]
aim to delineate the optimization and generalization dynamics of transformers. However, their findings usually apply under strict statistical assumptions about the data, while our study offers a comprehensive optimization-theoretic analysis of the attention model, establishing a formal linkage to max-margin problems and SVM geometry. This allows our findings to encompass the problem geometry and apply to diverse datasets. Overall, the max-margin equivalence provides a fundamental comprehension of the optimization geometry of transformers, offering a framework for prospective research endeavors, as outlined in the subsequent section.

9
Discussion, Future Directions, and Open Problems

Our optimization-theoretic characterization of the self-attention model provides a comprehensive understanding of its underlying principles. The developed framework, along with the research presented in
[
TLZO23
]
, introduces new avenues for studying transformers and language models. The key findings include:

\checkmark

The optimization geometry of self-attention exhibits a fascinating connection to hard-margin SVM problems. By leveraging linear constraints formed through outer products of token pairs, optimal input tokens can be effectively separated from non-optimal ones.

\checkmark

When gradient descent is employed without early-stopping, implicit regularization and convergence of self-attention naturally occur. This convergence leads to the maximum margin solution when minimizing specific requirements using logistic loss, exp-loss, or other smooth decreasing loss functions. Moreover, this implicit bias is unaffected by the step size, as long as it is sufficiently small for convergence, and remains independent of the initialization process.

The fact that gradient descent leads to a maximum margin solution may not be surprising to those who are familiar with the relationship between regularization path and gradient descent in linear and nonlinear neural networks
[
SHN+18
,
GLSS18
,
NLG+19
,
JT21
,
MWG+20
,
JT20
]
. However, there is a lack of prior research or discussion regarding this connection to the attention mechanism. Moreover, there has been no rigorous analysis or investigation into the exactness and independence of this bias with respect to the initialization and step size. Thus, we believe our findings and insights deepen our understanding of transformers and language models, paving the way for further research in this domain. Below, we discuss some notable directions and highlight open problems that are not resolved by the existing theory.

•

Convergence Rates
: The current paper establishes asymptotic convergence of gradient descent; nonetheless, there is room for further exploration to characterize non-asymptotic convergence rates. Indeed, such an exploration can also provide valuable insights into the choice of learning rate, initialization, and the optimization method.

•

Gradient descent on
({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})
parameterization:
We find it remarkable that regularization path analysis was able to predict the implicit bias of gradient descent. Complete analysis of gradient descent is inherently connected to the fundamental question of low-rank factorization
[
GWB+17
,
LMZ18
]
. We believe formalizing the implicit bias of gradient descent under margin constraints presents an exciting open research direction for further research.

•

Generalization analysis:
An important direction is the generalization guarantees for gradient-based algorithms. The established connection to hard-margin SVM can facilitate this because the SVM problem is amenable to statistical analysis. This would be akin to how kernel/NTK analysis for deep nets enabled a rich literature on generalization analysis for traditional deep learning.

•

Global convergence of gradient descent:
We lack a complete characterization of the directional convergence of gradient descent. We ask:
Where does gradient descent directionally-converge from arbitrary initialization for 1-layer self-attention?
The role of over-parameterization as conjectured in Section
5.2
and the notion of locally-optimal directions discussed in Section
5
constitute important pieces of this puzzle (also see the discussion in
[
TLZO23
]
).

•

Realistic architectures:
Naturally, we wish to explore whether max-margin equivalence can be extended to more realistic settings: Can the theory be expanded to handle multi-head attention, multi-layer architectures, and MLP nonlinearities? We believe the results in Section
6
take an important step towards this direction by including analytical formulae for the implicit bias of the attention layer under nonlinear prediction heads.

•

Jointly optimizing attention and prediction head:
It would be interesting to study the joint optimization dynamics of attention weights and prediction head
h(\cdot)
. This problem can be viewed as a novel low-rank factorization type problem where
h(\cdot)
and
\bm{W}
are factors of the optimization problem, only, here,
\bm{W}
passes through the softmax nonlinearity. To this aim,
[
TLZO23
]
provides a preliminary geometric characterization of the implicit bias for a simpler attention model using regularization path analysis. Such findings can potentially be generalized to the analysis of gradient methods and full transformer block.

Roadmap.

The appendix is organized as follows:

•

Appendix
A
provides the proof of Theorem
1
.

•

Appendix
B
provides auxiliary lemmas about the training risk.

•

Appendix
C
presents the proofs for the global convergence of gradient descent (Section
4
).

•

Appendix
D
presents the proofs for the local convergence of gradient descent (Section
5
).

•

Appendix
E
provides a general regularization path analysis. This analysis addresses the inductive bias of the attention layer for general norm objectives and beyond-linear prediction heads under a sequence-to-sequence classification model. The seq2seq aspect also goes beyond our results in the main body where we predict using single output token (Sections
3
and
5.4
).

•

Appendix
F
provides additional experiments and their discussion.

Appendix A
Proof of Theorem
1
: Separability Under Mild Over-Parameterization

We denote Kronecker product of two matrices via
\otimes
. Additionally, given
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, let us denote its vectorization
\bm{w}=\text{vec}(\bm{W})\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
. We first note that separation is implied by the linear independence of the constraints. Specifically, we are interested in guaranteeing

\left<\bm{w},\bm{f}_{it}\right>\geq 1\quad\text{for all}\quad i\in[n],~~t\neq{\bm{\alpha}}_{i},~~\textnormal{where}~~\bm{f}_{it}:=(\bm{x}_{i{\bm{\alpha}}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it})\otimes{\bm{z}}_{i}.

Note that, the inequality constraints above are feasible as soon as
\bm{f}_{it}
’s are linearly independent. Thus, we will instead prove linear independence of the vectors
(\bm{f}_{it})_{i\in[n],t\neq{\bm{\alpha}}_{i}}
. Also note that, since there are finitely many
{\bm{\alpha}}
choices, if we show almost sure separation for a fixed but arbitrary
{\bm{\alpha}}
choice, through union bound, we recover the result for all
{\bm{\alpha}}
. Thus, we prove the result for a fixed
{\bm{\alpha}}
choice.

We will prove this result inductively. Let
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)(T-1)\times d^{2}}
denote the matrix whose rows are given by the features
(\bm{f}_{it})_{i\in[n-1],t\neq{\bm{\alpha}}_{i}}
. Suppose the result is correct for
n-1
, thus,
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}
is full row-rank almost surely (post random Gaussian perturbation). Now, fix
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}
and, conditioned on
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}
being full row-rank, let us show that
{\bm{M}}_{n}
is also full row-rank almost surely. To prove this, consider the
n
’th example
({\bm{X}}_{n},{\bm{z}}_{n})
. Let
(\bm{g}_{t})_{t=1}^{T},\bm{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be random vectors with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
entries. Consider the perturbed input
{\bm{X}}^{\prime}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times d}
with tokens
\bm{x}^{\prime}_{nt}=\bm{x}_{nt}+\bm{g}_{t}
and
{\bm{z}}^{\prime}_{n}={\bm{z}}_{n}+\bm{h}
. Note that for self-attention, we set
{\bm{z}}_{n}=\bm{x}_{n1}
and
\bm{h}=\bm{g}_{1}
. From these, create the matrix
\tilde{{\bm{M}}}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{(T-1)\times d^{2}}
with rows
(\bm{f}^{\prime}_{nt})_{t\neq{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}}
where
\bm{f}^{\prime}_{nt}=(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{n{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}}-\bm{x}^{\prime}_{nt})\otimes{\bm{z}}^{\prime}_{n}
. Observe that
{\bm{M}}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{{\bm{M}}}_{n}\\
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}
. To conclude with the result, we will apply Lemma
7
. To apply this lemma, we have two claims.

Claim 1:
Let
\bar{{\bm{z}}}_{n}
be the projection of
{\bm{z}}^{\prime}_{n}
on the orthogonal complement of
({\bm{z}}_{i})_{i=1}^{n-1}
. Consider the matrix
\bar{{\bm{M}}}_{n}
with rows
\bar{\bm{f}}_{nt}=(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{n{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}}-\bm{x}^{\prime}_{nt})\otimes\bar{{\bm{z}}}_{n}
for
t\neq{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}
.
\bar{{\bm{M}}}_{n}
is rank
T-1
almost surely whenever
d\geq\max(T-1,n)
.

To see this claim, first denote the orthogonal complement of the span of the vectors
({\bm{z}}_{i})_{i=1}^{n-1}
by
Z_{n-1}
. The span of the vectors
({\bm{z}}_{i})_{i=1}^{n-1}
is at most
n-1
dimensional and, since
d\geq n
,
\text{dim}(Z_{n-1})\geq 1
. Consequently,
\bar{{\bm{z}}}_{n}\neq 0
almost surely because the Gaussian variable
{\bm{z}}_{n}+\bm{h}
will have nonzero projection on
Z_{n-1}
almost surely. Secondly, let
\bar{{\bm{X}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(T-1)\times d}
be the matrix whose rows are equal to
\bm{x}^{\prime}_{n{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}}-\bm{x}^{\prime}_{nt}
for
t\neq{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}
.
\bar{{\bm{X}}}
is full row-rank almost surely, this is because conditioned on
\bm{g}_{{\bm{\alpha}}_{n}}
, the matrix
\bar{{\bm{X}}}
is written as
\bar{{\bm{X}}}=\tilde{{\bm{X}}}+{\bm{G}}
where
\tilde{{\bm{X}}}
is deterministic and
{\bm{G}}
is i.i.d. Gaussian. The latter perturbation ensures full row-rank almost surely whenever
T-1\leq d
. Finally, note that
\bar{{\bm{M}}}_{n}=\bar{{\bm{X}}}\otimes\bar{{\bm{z}}}_{n}
. Since the rank of the Kronecker product is multiplicative, we conclude with the claim.

Claim 2:
Let
S_{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
be the null space of
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}
. There exists a subspace
P\subseteq S_{n-1}
such that rows of
\bar{{\bm{M}}}_{n}
are projections of the rows of
{\bm{M}}_{n}
on
P
, that is,
\bar{\bm{f}}_{nt}=\Pi_{P}(\bm{f}^{\prime}_{nt})
where
\Pi
denotes set projection.

To show this claim, let us consider the matrix forms of the vectorized features i.e. let us work with
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
rather than
\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
. Denote the notation change as
\bm{F}_{it}=(\bm{x}_{i{\bm{\alpha}}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top}\leftrightarrow\bm{f}_{it}=(\bm{x}_{i{\bm{\alpha}}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it})\otimes{\bm{z}}_{i}
. Recall that
Z_{n-1}
denotes the orthogonal complement of
({\bm{z}}_{i})_{i=1}^{n-1}
. Define
Q
to be the set of matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
whose column space lies in
Z_{n-1}
and
P
to be the vectorization of
Q
. We first show that
P
is a subset of the null space of
S_{n-1}
. To see this, fix any matrix
{\bm{A}}\in P
and a row
\bm{f}_{it}
from
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}
. Matricized
\bm{f}_{it}
can be written as
\bm{F}_{it}={\bm{a}}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top}
for
{\bm{z}}_{i}\in Z_{n-1}^{\perp}
. Since
{\bm{A}}\in Q
, this implies
\left<\bm{F}_{it},{\bm{A}}\right>={\bm{a}}^{\top}{\bm{A}}{\bm{z}}_{i}=0
as
{\bm{A}}{\bm{z}}_{i}=0
. This holds for all
\bm{F}_{it}
, thus,
\texttt{vectorized}({\bm{A}})\in\texttt{null}(S_{n-1})
.

Next, we need to show that
\bar{\bm{f}}_{nt}
is the projection of
\bm{f}^{\prime}_{nt}
on
P
. To see this, we will show that
\bar{\bm{f}}_{nt}\in P
whereas
\bm{f}^{\prime}_{nt}-\bar{\bm{f}}_{nt}\in P^{\perp}
for all
t
. Write
\bm{F}^{\prime}_{nt}=\texttt{matricized}(\bm{f}^{\prime}_{nt})={\bm{a}}{{\bm{z}}^{\prime}}_{n}^{\top}
. We have that
\bar{\bm{F}}_{nt}=\texttt{matricized}(\bar{\bm{f}}_{nt})={\bm{a}}\bar{{\bm{z}}}_{n}^{\top}
where
\bar{{\bm{z}}}_{n}=\Pi_{Z_{n-1}}({\bm{z}}^{\prime}_{n})
. This implies
\bar{\bm{F}}_{nt}\in Q
and
\bar{\bm{f}}_{nt}\in P
. Similarly, since
{\bm{z}}^{\prime}_{n}-\bar{{\bm{z}}}_{n}\in Z_{n-1}^{\perp}
which implies
\bm{F}^{\prime}_{nt}-\bar{\bm{F}}_{nt}\in Q^{\perp}
.

To conclude with the proof, observe that, through Claims 1 and 2,
{\bm{M}}_{n}=\begin{bmatrix}\tilde{{\bm{M}}}_{n}\\
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}\end{bmatrix}
satisfies the requirements of Lemma
7
almost surely, namely, projection of
\tilde{{\bm{M}}}_{n}
onto a subset of the null space of
{\bm{M}}_{n-1}
being full rank. Thus,
{\bm{M}}_{n}
is full rank almost surely.

\hfill\blacksquare

Lemma 7

Let
{\bm{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p},{{\bm{B}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times p}
. Suppose
n+m\leq p
and
{\bm{A}}
is full row-rank. Denote the null space of
{\bm{A}}
by
S_{\bm{A}}^{\perp}
. Let
P
be a subspace that is its subset i.e.
P\subseteq S_{\bm{A}}^{\perp}
. Let
{{\bm{B}}}^{\prime}
be the matrix obtained by projecting each of row of
{{\bm{B}}}
on
P
and suppose
{{\bm{B}}}^{\prime}
is full rank. Then, the concatenation
{\bm{C}}=[{\bm{A}};{{\bm{B}}}]

is full row-rank.

Proof.
Let
({\bm{a}}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
,
(\bm{b}_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
,
(\bm{b}^{\prime}_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
be the rows of
{\bm{A}},{{\bm{B}}},{{\bm{B}}}^{\prime}
, respectively. Suppose the set of rows of
{\bm{A}}
and
{{\bm{B}}}
are linearly dependent. Then, for some
(c_{i})_{i=1}^{n},(c^{\prime}_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
(which are not all-zeros), we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}{\bm{a}}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}c^{\prime}_{i}\bm{b}_{i}=0.

(13)

We now rewrite this as follows to decouple
P
and
P^{\perp}
:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}{\bm{a}}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}c^{\prime}_{i}\bm{b}^{\prime}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}c^{\prime}_{i}(\bm{b}^{\prime}_{i}-\bm{b}_{i})=0.

Projecting above inequality to
P
, we find that
\sum_{i=1}^{m}c^{\prime}_{i}\bm{b}^{\prime}_{i}=0
. Since
(\bm{b}^{\prime}_{i})_{i=1}^{m}
are linearly independent, we find
c^{\prime}_{i}=0
for all
i\in[m]
. This implies
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}{\bm{a}}_{i}=0
. Since
({\bm{a}}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
are linearly independent, this implies
c_{i}=0
for all
i\in[n]
. Thus, (
13
) can only hold if all coefficients are zero which is a contradiction.

Appendix B
Auxiliary Lemmas

B.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Let
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}
denote either solution of (
Att-SVM
) or (
Att-SVM⋆
). We claim that
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}
is at most rank
n
.
Suppose the claim is wrong and row space of
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}
does not lie within
\mathcal{S}=\texttt{span}(\{{\bm{z}}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})
. Let
\bm{W}=\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond})
denote the matrix obtained by projecting the rows of
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}
on
\mathcal{S}
. Observe that
\bm{W}
satisfies all SVM constraints since
\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i}=\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}{\bm{z}}_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
. For Frobenius norm, using
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}\neq\bm{W}
, we obtain a contradiction via
\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}}\|_{F}^{2}=\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}^{2}+\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}-\bm{W}}\|_{F}^{2}>\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}^{2}
. For nuclear norm, we can write
\bm{W}={\bm{U}}{\bm{{\Sigma}}}{\bm{V}}^{\top}
with
{\bm{{\Sigma}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
where
r
is dimension of
\mathcal{S}
and
\texttt{column\_span}({\bm{V}})=\mathcal{S}
.

To proceed, we split the problem into two scenarios.

Scenario 1:
Let
{\bm{U}}_{\perp},{\bm{V}}_{\perp}
be orthogonal complements of
{\bm{U}},{\bm{V}}
– viewing matrices with orthonormal columns as subspaces. Suppose
{\bm{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}{\bm{V}}_{\perp}\neq 0
. Then, singular value inequalities (which were also used in earlier works on nuclear norm analysis
[
RXH11
,
OH10
,
OMFH11
]
) guarantee that
\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}}\|_{\star}\geq\|{{\bm{U}}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}{\bm{V}}}\|_{\star}+\|{{\bm{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}{\bm{V}}_{\perp}}\|_{\star}>\|{\bm{W}}\|_{\star}
.

Scenario 2:
Now suppose
{\bm{U}}_{\perp}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}{\bm{V}}_{\perp}=0
. Since
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}{\bm{V}}_{\perp}\neq 0
, this implies
{\bm{U}}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}{\bm{V}}_{\perp}\neq 0
. Let
\bm{W}^{\prime}={\bm{U}}{\bm{U}}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}
which is a rank-
r
matrix. Since
\bm{W}^{\prime}
is a subspace projection, we have
\|{\bm{W}^{\prime}}\|_{\star}\leq\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}}\|_{\star}
. Next, observe that
\|{\bm{W}}\|_{\star}=\texttt{trace}({\bm{U}}^{\top}\bm{W}{\bm{V}})=\texttt{trace}({\bm{U}}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\prime}{\bm{V}})
. On the other hand,
\texttt{trace}({\bm{U}}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\prime}{\bm{V}})<\|{\bm{W}^{\prime}}\|_{\star}
because the equality in
von Neumann’s trace inequality
happens if and only if the two matrices we are inner-producting, namely
(\bm{W}^{\prime},{\bm{U}}{\bm{V}}^{\top})
, share a joint set of singular vectors
[
Car21
]
. However, this is not true as the row space of
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}
does not lie within
\mathcal{S}
. Thus, we obtain
\|{\bm{W}}\|_{\star}<\|{\bm{W}^{\prime}}\|_{\star}\leq\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\diamond}}\|_{\star}
concluding the proof via contradiction.
\hfill\blacksquare

B.2
Proof of Lemma
2

We first show that
{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})>{\cal{L}}_{\star}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}})
. The token at the output of the attention layer is given by
{\bm{a}}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}^{\top}\bm{s}_{i}
, where
\mathbb{S}({\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i})=\bm{s}_{i}
. Here,
{\bm{a}}_{i}
can be written as
{\bm{a}}_{i}=\sum_{t\in[T]}\bm{s}_{it}\bm{x}_{it}
, where
\bm{s}_{it}\geq 0
and
\sum_{t\in[T]}\bm{s}_{it}=1
. To proceed, using the linearity of
h(\bm{x})=\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{x}
, we find that

\displaystyle{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h({\bm{a}}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(Y_{i}\cdot\sum_{t\in[T]}\bm{s}_{it}h(\bm{x}_{it}))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}))=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}})={\cal{L}}_{\star}.

(14)

Here, the inequality follows since
\bm{\gamma}_{it}=Y_{i}\cdot h(\bm{x}_{it})=Y_{i}\cdot\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{x}_{it}\leq\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}
by Definition
1
and strictly-decreasing nature of the loss
\ell
due to Assumption
A
.

On the other hand, since not all tokens are optimal, there exists a token index
(i,t)
for which
Y_{i}\cdot h(\bm{x}_{it})<Y_{i}\cdot h(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}})
. Since all softmax entries obey
\bm{s}_{it}>0
for finite
\bm{W}
, this implies the strict inequality
\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h({\bm{a}}_{i}))>\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}))
. This leads to the desired conclusion
{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})>{\cal{L}}_{\star}
.

Next, we show that if (
Att-SVM
) is feasible i.e. there exists a
\bm{W}
separating some optimal indices
(\texttt{opt}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
from the other tokens, then
\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}{\cal{L}}(R\cdot\bm{W})={\cal{L}}_{\star}
. Note that, this assumption does not exclude the existence of other optimal indices. This implies that, letting
\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{S}({\bm{X}}_{i}(R\cdot\bm{W}){\bm{z}}_{i})
saturates the softmax and will be equal to the indicator function at
\texttt{opt}_{i}
for all inputs
i\in[n]
. Thus,
\bm{s}_{it}\rightarrow 0
for
t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}
and
\bm{s}_{it}\rightarrow 1
for
t=\texttt{opt}_{i}
. Using
M_{1}
-Lipschitzness of
\ell
, we can write

\left|\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}))-\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h({\bm{a}}_{i}))\right|\leq M_{1}\left|h({\bm{a}}_{i})-h(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}})\right|.

Since
h
is linear, it is
\|{\bm{v}}\|
-Lipschitz implying

\displaystyle\left|\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}))-\ell(Y_{i}\cdot h({\bm{a}}_{i}))\right|\leq M_{1}\|{\bm{v}}\|\cdot\|{{\bm{a}}_{i}-\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}}\|.

Since
{\bm{a}}_{i}\rightarrow\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}
as
R\rightarrow\infty
, (
B.2
) gives
\lim_{R\rightarrow\infty}{\cal{L}}(R\cdot\bm{W})={\cal{L}}_{\star}
.

\hfill\blacksquare

B.3
Proof of Lemma
4

Let

\bm{\gamma}_{i}=Y_{i}\cdot{\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{v},\quad\bm{h}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i}.

From Assumption
A
,
\ell:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is differentiable. Hence, the gradient evaluated at
\bm{W}
is given by

\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{X}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},

(15)

where

\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h})=\text{diag}\left(\mathbb{S}(\bm{h})\right)-\mathbb{S}(\bm{h})\mathbb{S}(\bm{h})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times T}.

(16)

Note that

\|\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h})\|\leq\|\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h})\|_{F}\leq 1.

(17)

Hence, for any
\bm{W},\dot{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
,
i\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})-\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i})\right\|\leq\left\|\bm{h}_{i}-\dot{\bm{h}}_{i}\right\|\leq\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|~\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|~\left\|\bm{W}-\dot{\bm{W}}\right\|_{F},

(18a)

where
\dot{\bm{h}}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\dot{\bm{W}}{\bm{z}}_{i}
.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i})-\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i})\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})-\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}_{i}})\right\|+\left\|\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})^{\top}-\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}_{i}})\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}_{i}})^{\top}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq 3\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|~\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|~\left\|\bm{W}-\dot{\bm{W}}\right\|_{F}.

(18b)

Next, for any
\bm{W},\dot{\bm{W}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, we get

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W})-\nabla\mathcal{L}(\dot{\bm{W}})\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}-\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}\right\|_{F}~\left|\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\right)-\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i})\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left|\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\right)\right|~\left\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}-{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{0}~\|\bm{\gamma}_{i}\|^{2}~\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|~\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|~\left\|\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})-\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i})\right\|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{1}~\|\bm{\gamma}_{i}\|~\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|~\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|~\left\|\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i})-\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i})\right\|_{F},

(19)

where the second inequality follows from the fact that
|ab-cd|\leq|d||a-c|+|a||b-d|
and the third inequality uses Assumption
A
and (
17
).

Substituting (
18a
) and (
B.3
) into (
B.3
), we get

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W})-\nabla\mathcal{L}(\dot{\bm{W}})\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(M_{0}~\|\bm{\gamma}_{i}\|^{2}\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|^{2}\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|^{2}+3M_{1}\|\bm{\gamma}_{i}\|~\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|^{2}~\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|^{2}\right)\|\bm{W}-\dot{\bm{W}}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(M_{0}~\|\bm{v}\|^{2}\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|^{2}\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|^{4}+3M_{1}\|\bm{v}\|~\|{\bm{z}}_{i}\|^{2}~\|{\bm{X}}_{i}\|^{3}\right)\|\bm{W}-\dot{\bm{W}}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\bm{W}}~\|\bm{W}-\dot{\bm{W}}\|_{F},

where
L_{\bm{W}}
is defined in (
3
).

Let
\bm{g}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}{\bm{K}}{\bm{Q}}^{\top}{\bm{z}}_{i}
. We have

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bm{K}}}{\cal{L}}({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{g}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{g}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}{\bm{Q}},

(20a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\bm{Q}}}{\cal{L}}({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{g}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{X}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{g}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\bm{K}}.

(20b)

By the similar argument as in (
B.3
), for any
{\bm{Q}}
and
\dot{\bm{Q}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{{\bm{Q}}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})-\nabla_{{\bm{Q}}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{K}},\dot{{\bm{Q}}})\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|{\bm{K}}\|}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\|\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}-\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{z}}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\dot{\bm{h}}_{i}){\bm{X}}_{i}\right\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq L_{\bm{W}}\|{\bm{K}}\|~\|{\bm{Q}}-\dot{{\bm{Q}}}\|_{F}.

(21)

Similarly, for any
{\bm{K}},\dot{\bm{K}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
, we get

\left\|\nabla_{{\bm{K}}}\mathcal{L}({\bm{K}},{\bm{Q}})-\nabla_{{\bm{K}}}\mathcal{L}(\dot{{\bm{K}}},{\bm{Q}})\right\|_{F}\leq L_{\bm{W}}\|{\bm{Q}}\|~\|{\bm{K}}-\dot{{\bm{K}}}\|_{F}.

\hfill\blacksquare

B.4
A useful lemma for gradient descent analysis

Lemma 8

For any
{\bm{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times d}
,
\bm{W},\bm{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and
{\bm{z}},\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
{\bm{a}}={\bm{X}}\bm{V}{\bm{z}}
,
\bm{s}=\mathbb{S}({\bm{X}}\bm{W}{\bm{z}})
, and
\bm{\gamma}={\bm{X}}\bm{v}
. Set

\Gamma=\sup_{t,\tau\in[T]}|\bm{\gamma}_{t}-\bm{\gamma}_{\tau}|~~~\textnormal{and}~~~A=\sup_{t\in[T]}\|{{\bm{a}}_{t}}\|.

We have that

\left|{\bm{a}}^{\top}\textnormal{diag}(\bm{s})\bm{\gamma}-{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{s}\bm{s}^{\top}\bm{\gamma}-\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}({\bm{a}}_{1}-{\bm{a}}_{t})\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\right|\leq 2\Gamma A(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
TLZO23
, Lemma 4]
, but for the sake of completeness, we provide it here. Set
\bar{\gamma}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{\gamma}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}
. We have

\displaystyle\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bar{\gamma}=\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\bm{s}_{t},~~\textnormal{and}~~|\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bar{\gamma}|\leq\Gamma(1-\bm{s}_{1}).

Then,

\displaystyle{\bm{a}}^{\top}\text{diag}(\bm{s})\bm{\gamma}-{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{s}\bm{s}^{\top}\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\bm{a}}_{t}\bm{\gamma}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}{\bm{a}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{\gamma}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}

\displaystyle={\bm{a}}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bar{\gamma})-\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}{\bm{a}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}(\bar{\gamma}-\bm{\gamma}_{t}).

(22)

Since

\left|\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}{\bm{a}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}(\bar{\gamma}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})-\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}{\bm{a}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\right|\leq A\Gamma(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2},

we obtain
5
5

5

For simplicity, we use
\pm
on the right hand side to denote the upper and lower bounds.

\displaystyle{\bm{a}}^{\top}\text{diag}(\bm{s})\bm{\gamma}-{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{s}\bm{s}^{\top}\bm{\gamma}

\displaystyle={\bm{a}}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bar{\gamma})-\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}{\bm{a}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\pm A\Gamma(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle={\bm{a}}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\bm{s}_{t}-\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}{\bm{a}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\pm A\Gamma(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}({\bm{a}}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}-{\bm{a}}_{t})\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\pm A\Gamma(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}({\bm{a}}_{1}-{\bm{a}}_{t})\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\pm 2A\Gamma(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}.

Here,
\pm
on the right handside uses the fact that

\left|\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}({\bm{a}}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}-{\bm{a}}_{1})\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\right|\leq(1-\bm{s}_{1})A\Gamma\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}\bm{s}_{t}=(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}A\Gamma.

Appendix C
Global Convergence of Gradient Descent

C.1
Divergence of norm of the iterates

The next lemma establishes the descent property of gradient descent for
\mathcal{L}(\bm{W})
under Assumption
A
.

Lemma 9
(Descent Lemma)

Under Assumption
A
, if
\eta\leq 1/L_{\bm{W}}
, then for any initialization
\bm{W}(0)
, Algorithm
W-GD
satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k+1))-\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))\leq-\frac{\eta}{2}\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))}\|_{F}^{2},

(23)

for all
k\geq 0
. Additionally, it holds that
\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}\left(\bm{W}(k)\right)}\|_{F}^{2}<\infty
, and
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}\left(\bm{W}\left(k\right)\right)}\|_{F}^{2}=0
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to
[
TLZO23
, Lemma 6]
.

The lemma below reveals that the correlation between the training loss’s gradient at any arbitrary matrix
\bm{W}
and the attention SVM solution
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
is negative. Consequently, for any finite
\bm{W}
,
\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right>
cannot be equal to zero.

Lemma 10

Let
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
be the SVM solution of (
Att-SVM
). Suppose Assumptions
A
and
B
hold. Then, for all
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, the training loss (
W-ERM
) obeys
\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right><0
.

Proof.

Let

\bm{\bar{h}}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}{\bm{z}}_{i},~~~\bm{\gamma}_{i}=Y_{i}\cdot{\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{v},~~~\textnormal{and}~~~\bm{h}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i}.

(24)

Let us recall the gradient evaluated at
\bm{W}
which is given by

\displaystyle\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\right)\cdot{\bm{X}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},

(25)

which implies that

\begin{split}\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right>&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}\left(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})\right)\cdot\left\langle{\bm{X}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right\rangle\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot{{\operatorname{trace}}}\left((\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})^{\top}{\bm{X}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}{\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\bm{\bar{h}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\bm{h}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}\\
&=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\left(\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}_{i}\text{diag}(\bm{s}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}-\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}_{i}\bm{s}_{i}\bm{s}^{\top}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}\right).\end{split}

(26)

Here, let
\ell^{\prime}_{i}:=\ell^{\prime}(\bm{\gamma}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i}))
,
\bm{s}_{i}=\mathbb{S}(\bm{h}_{i})
and the third equality uses
{{\operatorname{trace}}}\left(\bm{b}{\bm{a}}^{\top}\right)={\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{b}
.

In order to move forward, we will establish the following result, with a focus on the equal score condition (Assumption
B.2
): Let
\gamma=\bm{\gamma}_{t\geq 2}
be a constant, and let
\bm{\gamma}_{1}
and
\bar{\bm{h}}_{1}
represent the largest indices of vectors
\bm{\gamma}
and
\bm{\bar{h}}
respectively. For any vector
\bm{s}
that satisfies
\sum_{t\in[T]}\bm{s}_{t}=1
and
\bm{s}_{t}>0
, we aim to prove that
\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}\text{diag}(\bm{s})\bm{\gamma}-\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}\bm{s}\bm{s}^{\top}\bm{\gamma}>0
. To demonstrate this, we proceed by writing the following:

\begin{split}\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}\text{diag}(\bm{s})\bm{\gamma}-\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}\bm{s}\bm{s}^{\top}\bm{\gamma}&=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\bm{h}}_{t}\bm{\gamma}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bar{\bm{h}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\bm{\gamma}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}\\
&=\left(\bar{\bm{h}}_{1}\bm{\gamma}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}+\gamma\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}\bar{\bm{h}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}\right)-\Big(\bm{\gamma}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}+\gamma(1-\bm{s}_{1})\Big)\left(\bar{\bm{h}}_{1}\bm{s}_{1}+\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}\bar{\bm{h}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}\right)\\
&=\bar{\bm{h}}_{1}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\gamma)\bm{s}_{1}(1-\bm{s}_{1})-(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\gamma)\bm{s}_{1}\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}\bar{\bm{h}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}\\
&=(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\gamma)(1-\bm{s}_{1})\bm{s}_{1}\left[\bar{\bm{h}}_{1}-\frac{\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}\bar{\bm{h}}_{t}\bm{s}_{t}}{\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}\bm{s}_{t}}\right]\\
&\geq(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\gamma)(1-\bm{s}_{1})\bm{s}_{1}(\bar{\bm{h}}_{1}-\max_{t\geq 2}\bar{\bm{h}}_{t}).\end{split}

(27)

To proceed, define

{\gamma}_{\textsl{gap}}^{i}=\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\max_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{\gamma}_{it}~~\textnormal{and}~~{\bar{h}}_{\textsl{gap}}^{i}=\bar{\bm{h}}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\max_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bar{\bm{h}}_{it}.

With these, we obtain

\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}_{i}\text{diag}(\bm{s}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}-\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}_{i}\bm{s}_{i}\bm{s}^{\top}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}\geq{\gamma}_{\textsl{gap}}^{i}{\bar{h}}_{\textsl{gap}}^{i}(1-\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}})\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}.

(28)

Note that

\begin{split}&{\bar{h}}_{\textsl{gap}}^{i}=\min_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}~(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it})^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}{\bm{z}}_{i}\geq 1,\\
&{\gamma}_{\textsl{gap}}^{i}=\min_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}~\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}>0,\\
&\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}(1-\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}})>0.\end{split}

Hence,

\min_{i\in[n]}\left\{\left(\min_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}~(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it})^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}{\bm{z}}_{i}\right)\cdot\left(\min_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}~\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}\right)\cdot\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}(1-\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}})\right\}>0.

(29)

It follows from (
28
) and (
29
) that

\min_{i\in[n]}\left\{\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}_{i}\text{diag}(\bm{s}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}-\bm{\bar{h}}^{\top}_{i}\bm{s}_{i}\bm{s}^{\top}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}\right\}>0.

(30)

Further, by Assumption
A
,
\ell^{\prime}_{i}<0
,
\ell^{\prime}
is continuous and the domain is bounded, the maximum is attained and negative, and thus

\max_{x}\ell^{\prime}(x)<0.

(31)

Hence, using (
30
) and (
31
) in (
26
), we obtain

\begin{split}\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right><0.\end{split}

(32)

In the scenario that Assumption
B.1
holds (all tokens are support),
\bm{\bar{h}}_{t}=\bm{x}_{it}^{\top}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}{\bm{z}}_{i}
is constant for all
t\geq 2
. Hence, following similar steps as in (
27
) completes the proof.

C.1.1
Proof of Theorem
3

It follows from Lemma
9
that under Assumption
A
,
\eta\leq 1/L_{\bm{W}}
, and for any initialization
\bm{W}(0)
, the gradient descent sequence
\bm{W}(k+1)=\bm{W}(k)-\eta\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))
satisfies
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}\left(\bm{W}\left(k\right)\right)}\|_{F}^{2}=0
.

Further, it follows from Lemma
10
that
\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right><0
for all
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
. Hence, for any finite
\bm{W}
,
\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right>
cannot be equal to zero. Therefore, there are no finite critical points
\bm{W}
, for which
\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W})=0
which contradicts Lemma
9
. This
implies that
\left\|\bm{W}\left(k\right)\right\|\rightarrow\infty
.

\hfill\blacksquare

C.2
Global convergence under good initial gradient

To ensure global convergence, we identify an assumption that prevents GD from getting trapped at suboptimal tokens that offer no scoring advantage compared to other choices. To establish a foundation for providing the convergence of GD to the globally optimal solution
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
, we present the following definitions. For parameters
\mu\in(0,1)
and
R>0
, consider the following subset of the sphere and its associated cone:

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}):=\left\{\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}~\Big|~\left<(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\frac{\bm{W}}{\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}}\right>\geq\frac{\mu}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\quad\textnormal{for all}\quad t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i},\quad i\in[n]\right\},

(33a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}):=\left\{\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})~\Big|~\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}\geq R\right\}.

(33b)

Note that the
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
definition is equivalent to the
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}
definition in (
4
) with a change of variable
\mu\leftarrow\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}\cdot\mu
.

Lemma 11

Suppose Assumption
A
holds and let
\texttt{opt}=(\texttt{opt}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be the unique globally-optimal indices with
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
denoting the
Att-SVM
solution. Define the margin
\Theta=1/\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
. Let
\bm{s}_{i}=\mathbb{S}({\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i})
. For any
\mu>0
, there exists a sufficiently large
\bar{R}_{\mu}={\cal{O}}(1/\mu)
(see (
41
)) such that:

L1.

There is no stationary point within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,\bar{R}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
, where
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,\bar{R}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
is defined in (
33
).

L2.

For all
\bm{V}\in\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
with
\|{\bm{V}}\|_{F}=\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
and
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,\bar{R}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
, there exist dataset dependent constants
C,c>0
such that

\displaystyle C\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}\right)\geq-\Big\langle\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{V}\Big\rangle\geq c\cdot\mu\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}\right)>0,

(34a)

\displaystyle-\left<\frac{\bm{V}}{\|{\bm{V}}\|_{F}},\frac{\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})}{\|{\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})}\|_{F}}\right>\geq\frac{c}{C}\cdot\frac{\Theta}{\bar{A}}>0,

(34b)

\displaystyle\|{\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})}\|_{F}\leq\bar{A}C\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}\right).

(34c)

Here,
\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}=(\mathbb{S}({\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i}))_{\texttt{opt}_{i}}
,
\bar{A}=\max_{i\in[n],t,\tau\in[T]}\|{\bm{x}_{it}-\bm{x}_{i\tau}}\|~\|{{\bm{z}}_{i}}\|
, and
\Theta=1/\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
.

Proof.
For simplicity let
R=\bar{R}_{\mu}
,
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
and

\begin{split}&A=\max_{i\in[n],t,\tau\in[T]}\frac{(\|{\bm{x}_{it}}\|\vee\|{\bm{x}_{it}-\bm{x}_{i\tau}}\|)\cdot\|{{\bm{z}}_{i}}\|}{\Theta}.\end{split}

(35)

The following inequalities hold for all
\bm{V}\in\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu},~\|{\bm{V}}\|_{F}=\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
and all
i\in[n],t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}
:

\begin{split}A\geq(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it})^{\top}\bm{V}{\bm{z}}_{i}&\geq\mu.\end{split}

(36)

To proceed, we write the gradient correlation following (
15
) and (
26
)

\displaystyle\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{V}\right>

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\left<\bm{h}_{i},\mathbb{S}^{\prime}(\tilde{\bm{h}}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}\right>,

(37)

where we denoted
\ell^{\prime}_{i}=\ell^{\prime}(Y_{i}\cdot\bm{v}^{\top}{\bm{X}}_{i}^{\top}\mathbb{S}(\tilde{\bm{h}}_{i}))
,
\bm{h}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{V}{\bm{z}}_{i}
,
\tilde{\bm{h}}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i}
,
\bm{s}_{i}=\mathbb{S}(\tilde{\bm{h}}_{i})
.

It follows from (
35
) that
A\geq\max_{i\in[n],t\in[T]}\|{\bm{h}_{it}}\|
. Using (
36
), we can bound the softmax probabilities
\bm{s}_{i}=\mathbb{S}(\tilde{\bm{h}}_{i})
as follows, for all
i\in[n]
:

\displaystyle S_{i}:=\sum_{\tau\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{i\tau}\leq Te^{-R\mu\Theta}\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}\leq Te^{-R\mu\Theta}.

(38)

Recall scores
\bm{\gamma}_{it}=Y_{i}\cdot\bm{v}^{\top}\bm{x}_{it}
. Define the score gaps:

\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}=\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\max_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{\gamma}_{it},~~~\bar{\gamma}^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}=\bm{\gamma}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\min_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{\gamma}_{it},~~~\textnormal{and}~~~\Gamma=\sup_{i\in[n],t,\tau\in[T]}|\bm{\gamma}_{it}-\bm{\gamma}_{i\tau}|.

Let us focus on a fixed datapoint
i\in[n]
, assume (without losing generality)
\texttt{opt}_{i}=1
, and drop subscripts
i
.
Directly applying Lemma
8
, we obtain

\big|\bm{h}^{\top}\text{diag}(\bm{s})\bm{\gamma}-\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{s}\bm{s}^{\top}\bm{\gamma}-\sum_{t\geq 2}^{T}(\bm{h}_{1}-\bm{h}_{t})\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\big|\leq 2\Gamma A(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}.

To proceed, let us upper/lower bound the gradient correlation. Since
A\geq\bm{h}_{1}-\bm{h}_{t}\geq\mu>0
from (
36
), setting
S:=\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{t}=1-\bm{s}_{1}
, we find

A\cdot S\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{\textsl{gap}}\geq\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}}(\bm{h}_{1}-\bm{h}_{t})\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\geq\mu\cdot S\cdot\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}.

(39)

Next we show that
S=1-\bm{s}_{1}
dominates
(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}=S^{2}
for large
R
. Specifically, we wish for

\displaystyle\mu S\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}/2\geq 2\Gamma A(1-\bm{s}_{1})^{2}\iff S\geq\frac{4}{\mu}\frac{\Gamma A}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}}S^{2}\iff S\leq\frac{\mu\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}}{4\Gamma A}.

(40)

Using (
38
), what we wish is ensured for all
i\in[n]
, by guaranteeing
Te^{-R\mu\Theta}\leq\frac{\mu\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}}{4\Gamma A}
. That is, by choosing

\displaystyle R\geq\frac{1}{\mu\Theta}\log\left(\frac{4T\Gamma A}{\mu\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}\right),

(41)

where
\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}=\min_{i\in[n]}\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}
is the global scalar corresponding to the worst case score gap over all inputs.

With the above choice of
R
, we guaranteed

2A(1-\bm{s}_{1})\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{\textsl{gap}}\geq 2A\cdot S\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{\textsl{gap}}\geq\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}}(\bm{h}_{1}-\bm{h}_{t})\bm{s}_{t}(\bm{\gamma}_{1}-\bm{\gamma}_{t})\geq\frac{\mu\cdot S\cdot\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}}{2}\geq\frac{\mu(1-\bm{s}_{1})\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}}{2},

via (
40
) and (
39
).

Since this holds over all inputs, going back to the gradient correlation (
37
) and averaging above over all inputs
i\in[n]
and plugging back the indices
i
, we obtain the advertised bound

\displaystyle\frac{2A}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}-\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot S_{i}\cdot\bar{\gamma}^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}\geq-\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{V}\right>\geq\frac{\mu}{2n}\sum_{i\in[n]}-\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot S_{i}\cdot\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}.

(42)

Let
-\ell^{\prime}_{\min/\max}
be the min/max values negative loss derivative admits over the ball
[-A,A]
and note that
\max_{i\in[n]}\bar{\gamma}^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}>0
and
\min_{i\in[n]}\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}>0
are dataset dependent constants. Then, we declare the constants
C=-2A\ell^{\prime}_{\max}\cdot\max_{i\in[n]}\bar{\gamma}^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}>0,c=-(1/2)\ell^{\prime}_{\min}\cdot\min_{i\in[n]}\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}>0
to obtain the bound (
34a
).

The proof of (
34b
) and (
34c
) follows similarly as the proof of Lemma
13
.

The following lemma shows that as
\pi
approaches zero, the negative gradient of the loss function at
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
becomes more correlated with the max-margin solution (
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
) than with
\bm{W}
itself.

Lemma 12

Suppose Assumption
A
holds and let
\texttt{opt}=(\texttt{opt}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be the unique optimal tokens with
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
denoting the SVM solution. Fix any
\mu>0
(per Lemma
11
). For any choice of
\pi>0
, there exists
R:=R_{\pi}\geq\bar{R}_{\mu}
such that, for any
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
, we have

\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\frac{\bm{W}}{\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}}\right>\geq(1+\pi)\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right>.

Here,
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
is the cone defined at (
33
).

Proof.

Let
{\bm{\bar{W}}}=\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}\bm{W}/\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}
,
\bm{h}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}{\bm{\bar{W}}}{\bm{z}}_{i}
,
\bm{\bar{h}}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}{\bm{z}}_{i}
, and
\bm{s}_{i}=\mathbb{S}({\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i})
. To establish the result, we will prove that, for sufficiently large
R
and for any
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
:

\displaystyle\left<-\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\frac{\bm{W}}{\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}}\right>

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\left<\bm{h}_{i},\mathbb{S}^{\prime}({\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}\right>

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1+\pi}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\left<\bm{\bar{h}}_{i},\mathbb{S}^{\prime}({\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{W}{\bm{z}}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}\right>=(1+\pi)\left<-\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right>.

(43)

Directly applying Lemma
8
, for all
\bm{V}\in\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}
with
\|{\bm{V}}\|_{F}=\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
and
\tilde{\bm{h}}_{i}={\bm{X}}_{i}\bm{V}{\bm{z}}_{i}
, we have found

\displaystyle\big|\tilde{\bm{h}}^{\top}_{i}\text{diag}(\bm{s}_{i})\bm{\gamma}_{i}-\tilde{\bm{h}}^{\top}_{i}\bm{s}_{i}\bm{s}^{\top}_{i}\bm{\gamma}_{i}-\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\tilde{\bm{h}}_{i1}-\tilde{\bm{h}}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\big|\leq 2\Gamma A(1-\bm{s}_{i1})^{2}.

(44)

Recalling
\bm{\bar{h}}_{i1}-\bm{\bar{h}}_{it}\geq 1
, we note that
\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\leq\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{\bar{h}}_{i1}-\bm{\bar{h}}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})
. Now plugging in
\bm{h},\bm{\bar{h}}
in the bound above and assuming
\pi\leq 1
(w.l.o.g.), (
C.2
) is implied by the following stronger inequality

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\left(6\Gamma A(1-\bm{s}_{i1})^{2}+\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\right)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1+\pi}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{\bar{h}}_{i1}-\bm{\bar{h}}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1+\pi}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}).

First, we claim that
0.5\pi\sum_{t\in\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\geq 6\Gamma A(1-\bm{s}_{i1})^{2}
for all
i\in[n]
.
The proof of this claim directly follows the argument in Lemma
11
, (namely following (
38
), (
40
), (
41
))
we have that
1-\bm{s}_{i1}\leq Te^{-R\mu\Theta}
and
\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}\geq\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}
for all
i\in[n]
. This leads to the choice (for
D_{0}\geq 12
)

\displaystyle R\geq R_{\pi}=\frac{1}{\mu\Theta}\log\left(\frac{D_{0}\cdot T\Gamma A}{\pi\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}\right).

(45)

We shall choose
D_{0}
sufficiently large such that
R_{\pi}\geq\bar{R}_{\mu}
, where
\bar{R}_{\mu}
is defined in Lemma
11
.

Following this control over the perturbation term
6\Gamma A(1-\bm{s}_{i1})^{2}
, to conclude with the result, what remains is proving the comparison

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\leq-\frac{1+0.5\pi}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell^{\prime}_{i}\cdot\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}).

(46)

Scenario 1:

\|{{\bm{\bar{W}}}-\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}\leq\epsilon=\frac{\pi}{4A\Theta}
for some
\epsilon>0
. In this scenario, for any
t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}
and
i\in[n]
, we have

|\bm{h}_{it}-\bm{\bar{h}}_{it}|=|\bm{x}_{it}^{\top}({\bm{\bar{W}}}-\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}){\bm{z}}_{i}|\leq A\Theta\epsilon=\frac{\pi}{4}.

Consequently, we obtain

\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it}\leq\bm{\bar{h}}_{i1}-\bm{\bar{h}}_{it}+2A\Theta\epsilon=1+0.5\pi.

Similarly,
\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it}\geq 1-0.5\pi\geq 0.5
. Since all terms
\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it},\bm{s}_{it},\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}
in (
46
) are nonnegative, we obtain (
46
).

Scenario 2:

\|{{\bm{\bar{W}}}-\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}\geq\epsilon=\frac{\pi}{4A\Theta}
. Since
{\bm{\bar{W}}}
is not max-margin solution, in this scenario, for some
i\in[n]
,
\nu=\nu(\epsilon)>0
, and
\tau\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}
, we have that

\displaystyle\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{i\tau}\leq 1-2\nu.

Here
\tau=\arg\max_{\tau\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{x}_{i\tau}{\bm{\bar{W}}}{\bm{z}}_{i}
denotes the nearest point to
\bm{h}_{i1}
(along the
{\bm{\bar{W}}}
direction). Recall that
\bm{s}=\mathbb{S}(\bar{R}\bm{h})
, where
\bar{R}=R\Theta=\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}/\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
. To proceed, let
\underline{\bm{h}}_{i}:=\min_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}:=\left\{i\in[n]:\underline{\bm{h}}_{i}\leq 1-2\nu\right\},\qquad[n]-\mathcal{I}:=\left\{i\in[n]:1-2\nu<\underline{\bm{h}}_{i}\right\}.

For all
i\in[n]-\mathcal{I}
,

\begin{split}\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})&-(1+0.5\pi)\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\\
&\leq\left(2A-(1+0.5\pi)\right)\Gamma\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i},~\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it}\geq 1+\frac{\pi}{2}}\bm{s}_{it}\\
&\leq\left(2A-(1+0.5\pi)\right)\Gamma Te^{-\bar{R}(1+\frac{\pi}{2})}\\
&\leq 2A\Gamma Te^{-\bar{R}(1+\frac{\pi}{2})}.\end{split}

(47)

For all
i\in\mathcal{I}
, split the tokens into two groups: Let
{\cal{N}}_{i}
be the group of tokens obeying
\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it}\leq 1-\nu
and
\bar{\cal{N}}_{i}:=[T]-\{\texttt{opt}_{i}\}-{\cal{N}}_{i}
be the rest of the neighbors. Observe that

\frac{\sum_{t\in\bar{{\cal{N}}}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}}{\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}}\leq T\frac{e^{\nu\bar{R}}}{e^{2\nu\bar{R}}}=Te^{-\bar{R}\nu}.

Using
|\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it}|\leq 2A
and
\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}=\min_{i\in[n]}\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{i}=\min_{i\in[n]}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\max_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{\gamma}_{it})
, observe that

\sum_{t\in\bar{\cal{N}}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\leq\frac{2\Gamma ATe^{-\bar{R}\nu}}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}).

Thus,

\displaystyle\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})

\displaystyle=\sum_{t\in{\cal{N}}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})+\sum_{t\in\bar{\cal{N}}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t\in{\cal{N}}_{i}}(1-\nu)\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})+\frac{2\Gamma ATe^{-\bar{R}\nu}}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\nu+\frac{2\Gamma ATe^{-\bar{R}\nu}}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}\right)\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{2\Gamma ATe^{-\bar{R}\nu}}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}\right)\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it}).

Hence, choosing

\displaystyle R\geq\frac{1}{\nu\Theta}\log\left(\frac{8\Gamma AT}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}\pi}\right)

(48)

results in that

\begin{split}&\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})-\left(1+\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\\
&\leq\left(\frac{2\Gamma ATe^{-\bar{R}\nu}}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\\
&\leq-\frac{\pi}{4}\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\\
&\leq-\frac{\pi}{4T}\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}e^{-\bar{R}(1-2\nu)}.\end{split}

(49)

Here, the last inequality follows from the fact that
\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}\geq\max_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}\bm{s}_{it}\geq\frac{e^{-\bar{R}(1-2\nu)}}{\sum_{t=1}^{T}e^{-\bar{R}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})}}\geq e^{-\bar{R}(1-2\nu)}/T
.

From Assumption
A
, we have
c_{\min}\leq-\ell^{\prime}\leq c_{\max}
for some positive constants
c_{\min}
and
c_{\max}
. It follows from (
47
) and (
49
) that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i}^{n}\ell_{i}^{\prime}\cdot

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(\bm{h}_{i1}-\bm{h}_{it})\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})-\sum_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i}}(1+0.5\pi)\bm{s}_{it}(\bm{\gamma}_{i1}-\bm{\gamma}_{it})\right)

\displaystyle\leq c_{\max}2A\Gamma T\Gamma e^{-\bar{R}(1+\frac{\pi}{2})}-\frac{c_{\min}}{nT}\cdot\frac{\pi\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}}{4}e^{-\bar{R}(1-2\nu)}

\displaystyle\leq 0.

Combing with (
48
), this is guaranteed by choosing

R\geq\max\left\{\frac{1}{\nu\Theta}\log\left(\frac{8\Gamma AT}{\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}\pi}\right),\frac{1}{(2\nu+\pi/2)\Theta}\log\left(\frac{8n\Gamma AT^{2}c_{\max}}{c_{\min}\gamma^{\textsl{gap}}_{\min}\pi}\right)\right\},

where
\nu=\nu(\frac{\pi}{4A\Theta})
depends only on
\pi
and global problem variables.

Combining this with the prior
R
choice (
45
) (by taking maximum), we conclude with the statement.

C.2.1
Proof of Theorem
4

The following theorem is a restatement of Theorem
4
. Two minor differences are: (1) We state with the change of variable
\mu\leftarrow\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}\cdot\mu
; see discussions below (
33
). (2) We also include (
L3.
) in the statement of the theorem.

Theorem 8

Suppose Assumption
A
on the loss function
\ell
and Assumption
C
on the initial gradient hold.

L1.

For any
\mu>0
, there exists
R>0
such that
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
defined in (
33
) does not contain any stationary points.

L2.

Fix any
\mu\in(0,\min(1,\iota\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}/\|{\nabla{\cal{L}}(0)}\|_{F})
. Consider GD iterations with
\bm{W}(0)=0
,
\bm{W}(1)=-R\nabla{\cal{L}}(0)/\|{\nabla{\cal{L}}(0)}\|_{F}
, and
\bm{W}(k+1)=\bm{W}(k)-\eta\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k))
for
\eta\leq 1/L_{\bm{W}}
,
k\geq 1
, and
R
sufficiently large. If all iterates remain within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}
, then
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}=\infty
and
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\bm{W}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}=\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}
.

L3.

Assume
\eta\leq 1/L_{\bm{W}}
and for all
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
with sufficiently large
R
?

\displaystyle\min_{i\in[n]}\left<(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\bm{W}-\eta\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})\right>\geq\min_{i\in[n]}\left<(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\bm{W}\right>-\frac{2\eta\mu}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}^{2}}\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}\right\rangle,

(50)

then all GD iterations remain within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
.

Proof.

Note that
L1.
is a direct corollary of Lemma
11
. We proceed with the proof of
L2.
and
L3.
.
We provide the proof in four steps:

Step 1:
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R^{0}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
construction
. Let us denote the initialization lower bound as
R^{0}_{\mu}:=R
, where
R
is given in the Theorem
8
’s statement. Consider an arbitrary value of
\epsilon\in(0,\mu/2)
and let
1/(1+\pi)=1-\epsilon
. We additionally denote
R_{\epsilon}\leftarrow R_{\pi}\vee 1/2
where
R_{\pi}
was defined in Lemma
12
. At initialization
\bm{W}(0)
, we set
\epsilon=\mu/2
to obtain
R^{0}_{\mu}=R_{\mu/2}
.

We proceed to show
\mu\in(0,\min(1,\iota\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}/\|{\nabla{\cal{L}}(0)}\|_{F})
. It follows from Assumption
C
and under zero initialization for GD (
\bm{W}(0)=0
) that

\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},-\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(0))\right\rangle=\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},-\nabla\mathcal{L}(0)\right\rangle\geq\iota>0,

for some positive constant
\iota
. Hence, for any initial step size
\eta(0)>0
and
\bm{W}(1)=-\eta(0)\nabla{\cal{L}}(0)
,

\begin{split}\left<(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\frac{\bm{W}(1)}{\|{\bm{W}(1)}\|_{F}}\right>&=\frac{\eta(0)}{\|{\bm{W}(1)}\|_{F}}\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},-\nabla\mathcal{L}(0)\right\rangle\\
&\geq\frac{\iota\eta(0)}{\|{\bm{W}(1)}\|_{F}}=\frac{\iota}{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(0)}\|_{F}}\\
&\geq\frac{\mu}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}.\end{split}

(51)

Here, the last inequality follows from our choice of
\mu
in the theorem statement, i.e.

\displaystyle\mu\in\left(0,\min\left(1,\frac{\iota\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(0)}\|_{F}}\right)\right).

(52)

This
\mu
choice induces the conic set
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R^{0}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
with
R^{0}_{\mu}=R_{\mu/2}
, where
R_{\mu/2}
was defined in Lemma
12
. Now, given the parameter
\mu
satisfying (
52
), we can choose
\eta(0)
such that
\|{\bm{W}(1)}\|_{F}\geq R^{0}_{\mu}
and
\bm{W}(1)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R^{0}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
. To achieve this, since
\bm{W}(0)=0
, we obtain

\eta(0)=\frac{R^{0}_{\mu}}{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(0)}\|_{F}}.

(53)

Since by our definition,
R^{0}_{\mu}\leftarrow R
, (
53
) gives
\bm{W}(1)
in the theorem’s statement.

Step 2: There are no stationary points within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\mu}^{0}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
.

This step follows from
L1.
. Specifically,
we can apply Lemma
11
to find that: For all
\bm{V},\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{\mu}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
with
\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}\neq 0
and
\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}\geq R^{0}_{\mu}
, we have that
-\left<\bm{V},\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W})\right>
is strictly positive.

Gradient correlation holds for large parameter norm.
It follows from Lemma
12
that, there exists
R_{\epsilon}\geq\bar{R}_{\mu}\vee 1/2
such that all
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
satisfy

\displaystyle\left\langle-\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}),\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle\geq(1-\epsilon)\left\langle-\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}),\frac{\bm{W}}{\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle.

(54)

The following argument applies to a general
\epsilon\in(0,\mu/2)
. However, at initialization
\bm{W}(0)=0
, we have set
\epsilon=\mu/2
and defined the initialization radius as
R^{0}_{\mu}=R_{\mu/2}
. To proceed, we will prove the main statements (
L2.
) and (
L3.
) as follows.

•

Proving
L3.
: In
Step 3
, we will assume Condition (
50
) to prove that gradient iterates remain within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
. Concretely, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\mu/2)
, we will show that after gradient descent enters the conic set
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
for the first time, it will never leave the set under Condition (
50
) of the theorem statement and (
54
). In what follows, let us denote
k_{\epsilon}
to be the first time gradient descent enters
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
. Note that for
\epsilon\leftarrow\mu/2
,
k_{\epsilon}=0
i.e. the point of initialization.

•

Proving
L2.
: In
Step 4
, assuming iterates within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
, we will prove that the norm diverges (as a result such
k_{\epsilon}
is guaranteed to exist) and, additionally, the gradient updates asymptotically aligns with
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
.

Step 3 (Proof of
L3.
): Updates remain inside the cone
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
.
Note that if
\bm{W}(k)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
for all
k\geq 1
, the required condition in
L2.
holds, and we proceed to
Step 4
. In this step, we show
L3.
. Specifically, we show that under Condition (
50
) and using (
54
), all iterates
\bm{W}(k)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
remain within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
.

To proceed, by leveraging the results from
Step 1
and
Step 2
, we demonstrate that the gradient iterates, with an appropriate constant step size, starting from
\bm{W}(k_{\epsilon})\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
, remain within this set. We proceed by induction. Suppose that the claim holds up to iteration
k\geq k_{\epsilon}
. This implies that
\bm{W}(k)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
. Hence, recalling
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
defined in (
33
), there exists scalar
\mu=\mu({\bm{\alpha}})\in(0,1)
and
R_{\epsilon}
such that
\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}\geq R_{\epsilon}
, and

\begin{split}\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\frac{\bm{W}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\right\rangle\geq\mu\Theta,\end{split}

where
\Theta=1/\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
.

Let

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left\langle{\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}},-\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))\right\rangle=:\rho(k)>0.

(55a)

Using (
50
), we have

\begin{split}\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\frac{\bm{W}(k+1)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\right\rangle&=\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\frac{\bm{W}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}-\frac{\eta}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))\right\rangle\\
&\geq\mu\Theta+\frac{2\eta(1-\epsilon)\mu\Theta\rho(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}.\end{split}

(56)

From Lemma
11
, we have
\left\langle\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k)),\bm{W}(k)\right\rangle<0
which implies that
\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}\geq\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}
. This together with
R_{\epsilon}
definition and
\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}\geq 1/2
implies that

\displaystyle\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{{2\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}}\left(\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}^{2}+\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\left(2\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}^{2}-2\eta\left\langle\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k)),\bm{W}(k)\right\rangle+\eta^{2}\|{\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k))}\|_{F}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}-\frac{\eta}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\left\langle\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k)),\bm{W}(k)\right\rangle+\eta^{2}\|\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k))\|_{F}^{2}.

Thus,

\begin{split}\frac{\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}&\leq 1-\frac{\eta}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\left\langle\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k)),\frac{\bm{W}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\right\rangle+\eta^{2}\frac{\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))\|_{F}^{2}}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\\
&\leq 1-\frac{\eta}{(1-\epsilon)\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\left\langle\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k)),\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle+\eta^{2}\frac{\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))\|_{F}^{2}}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\\
&\leq 1+\frac{\eta\rho(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}+\frac{\eta^{2}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))\|_{F}^{2}}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}=:C_{1}(\rho(k),\eta).\end{split}

(57)

Here, the second inequality uses (
54
).

Now, it follows from (
56
) and (
57
) that

\begin{split}\min_{t\neq\texttt{opt}_{i},~i\in[n]}~~\left\langle(\bm{x}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}-\bm{x}_{it}){\bm{z}}_{i}^{\top},\frac{\bm{W}(k+1)}{\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}}\right\rangle&\geq\frac{1}{C_{1}({\rho}(k),\eta)}\left(\mu\Theta+\frac{2\eta(1-\epsilon)\mu\Theta{\rho}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\right)\\
&=\mu\Theta+\frac{\eta\mu\Theta}{C_{1}({\rho}(k),\eta)}\left(\frac{\big(2(1-\epsilon)-1\big)\rho(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}-\eta\frac{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))}\|_{F}^{2}}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\right)\\
&=\mu\Theta+\frac{\eta\mu\Theta}{C_{1}({\rho}(k),\eta)}\left(\frac{(1-2\epsilon)\rho(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}-\eta\frac{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))}\|_{F}^{2}}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\right)\\
&\geq\mu\Theta,\end{split}

(58)

where the last inequality uses our choice of stepsize
\eta\leq 1/L_{W}
in Theorem
4
’s statement. Specifically, we need
\eta
to be small to ensure the last inequality. We will guarantee this by choosing a proper
R_{\epsilon}
in Lemma
12
. Specifically, Lemma
12
leaves the choice of
D_{0}
in
R_{\epsilon}
lower bound of (
45
) open (it can always be chosen larger). Here, by choosing
D_{0}\gtrsim 1/L_{\bm{W}}
will ensure
\eta\leq 1/L_{\bm{W}}
works well.

\begin{split}\eta&\leq\big(1-\mu\big)\mu\frac{c}{C}\frac{\Theta}{\bar{A}}\frac{1}{\bar{A}CT}e^{R_{\mu}^{0}\Theta/2}\\
&\leq\frac{1-2\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\frac{c\mu}{C}\frac{\Theta}{\bar{A}}\frac{1}{\bar{A}CT}e^{R_{\mu}^{0}\Theta/2}\\
&\leq\big(1-2\epsilon\big)\frac{\rho(k)}{\|\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))\|^{2}_{F}}.\end{split}

(59)

Here, the first inequality follows since
\epsilon\in(0,\mu/2)
(as seen in
Step 2
). Also,
\mu<1
implies that
1-\mu>0
, we obtain
\eta>0
. The last inequality is obtained from Lemma
11
:

\displaystyle\frac{\rho(k)}{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))}\|_{F}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left\langle\frac{\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))}{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))}\|_{F}},\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle\geq\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\cdot\frac{c\mu}{C}\cdot\frac{\Theta}{\bar{A}},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\|{\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))}\|_{F}}

\displaystyle{\geq\frac{1}{\bar{A}C\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(1-\bm{s}_{i\texttt{opt}_{i}}\right)}\geq\frac{1}{\bar{A}CTe^{-R_{\mu}^{0}\Theta/2}}}

for some data dependent constrants
c
,
C
,
\bar{A}=\max_{i\in[n],t,\tau\in[T]}\|{(\bm{x}_{it}-\bm{x}_{i\tau})}\|~\|{{\bm{z}}_{i}}\|
, and
\Theta=1/\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
.

The remainder of the proof of this step is identical to (
86
)–(
90
), with the replacement of
C_{0}
by
D_{0}
and the tracking of changes. Specifically, Lemma
12
leaves the choice of
D_{0}
in
R_{\epsilon}
lower bound of (
45
) open (it can always be chosen larger). Hence, for sufficiently large
D_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\eta\leq\frac{1}{L_{\bm{W}}}\leq\big(1-\mu\big)\mu\frac{c}{C}\frac{\Theta}{\bar{A}}\frac{1}{\bar{A}CT}e^{R_{\mu}^{0}\Theta/2}.

(60)

This implies (
58
) and
\bm{W}(k+1)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
.

Step 4 (Proof of
L2.
):
\bm{W}(k)
and
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}
perfectly align over time.

By theorem statement (alternatively via
Step 3
), we have that all iterates remain within the initial conic set i.e.
\bm{W}(k)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R^{0}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
for all
k\geq 0
. Note that it follows from Lemma
11
that
\left<\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}),\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}/\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}\right><0
, for any finite
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R^{0}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
. Hence, there are no finite critical points
\bm{W}\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R^{0}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
, for which
\nabla\mathcal{L}(\bm{W})=0
. Now, based on Lemma
9
, which guarantees that
\nabla{\cal{L}}(\bm{W}(k))\rightarrow 0
, this
implies that
\left\|\bm{W}\left(k\right)\right\|\rightarrow\infty
. Consequently, for any choice of
\epsilon\in(0,\mu/2)
there is an iteration
k_{\epsilon}
such that, for all
k\geq k_{\epsilon}
,
\bm{W}(k)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
. Once within
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
, multiplying both sides of (
54
) by the stepsize
\eta
and using the gradient descent update, we get

\begin{split}\left\langle\bm{W}(k+1)-\bm{W}(k),\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle&\geq(1-\epsilon)\left\langle\bm{W}(k+1)-\bm{W}(k),\frac{\bm{W}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\right\rangle\\
&=\frac{(1-\epsilon)}{2\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\left(\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}^{2}-\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}^{2}-\|{\bm{W}(k+1)-\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}^{2}\right)\\
&\geq(1-\epsilon)\left(\frac{1}{2\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}}\left(\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}^{2}-\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}^{2}\right)-\|{\bm{W}(k+1)-\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}^{2}\right)\\
&\geq(1-\epsilon)\left(\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}-\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}-\|{\bm{W}(k+1)-\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}^{2}\right)\\
&\geq(1-\epsilon)\Big(\|{\bm{W}(k+1)}\|_{F}-\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}-2\eta\left(\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k))-\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k+1))\right)\Big).\end{split}

Here, the second inequality is obtained from
\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}\geq 1/2
; the third inequality follows since for any
a,b>0
, we have
(a^{2}-b^{2})/(2b)-(a-b)\geq 0
; and the last inequality uses Lemma
9
.

Summing the above inequality over
k\geq k_{\epsilon}
gives

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{\bm{W}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}},\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle\geq 1-\epsilon+\frac{C(\epsilon,\eta)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}},\qquad\bm{W}(k)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}),

where
\mathcal{L}_{\star}\leq\mathcal{L}\left(\bm{W}\left(k\right)\right)
for all
k\geq k_{\epsilon}
, and

C(\epsilon,\eta)=\left\langle\bm{W}(k_{\epsilon}),\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle-(1-\epsilon)\|{\bm{W}(k_{\epsilon})}\|_{F}-2\eta(1-\epsilon)(\mathcal{L}(\bm{W}(k_{\epsilon}))-\mathcal{L}_{\star}).

Consequently,

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}\left\langle\frac{\bm{W}(k)}{\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}},\frac{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}{\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle\geq 1-\epsilon,\qquad\bm{W}(k)\in\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\mu,R_{\epsilon}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}).

Since
\epsilon\in(0,\mu/2)
is arbitrary, this implies
\bm{W}(k)/\|{\bm{W}(k)}\|_{F}\to\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}/\|{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}}\|_{F}
.

Appendix D
Local Convergence of Gradient Descent

To provide a basis for discussing local convergence of GD, we establish a cone centered around
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}}
using the following construction. For parameters
\mu\in(0,1)
and
R>0
, we define
\mathcal{C}_{\mu,R}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}})
as the set of matrices
\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
such that
\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}\geq R
and the correlation coefficient between
\bm{W}
and
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}}
is at least
1-\mu
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mu}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}~:~\left\langle\frac{\bm{W}}{\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}},\frac{{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}}}}{\|{{\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}}}}\|_{F}}\right\rangle\geq 1-\mu\right\},

(61a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\mu,R}({\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}}})

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{S}_{\mu}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}})\cap\left\{\bm{W}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}~:~\|{\bm{W}}\|_{F}\geq R\right\}.

(61b)

Lemma 13

Suppose Assumption
A
on the loss function
\ell
holds, and let
{\bm{\alpha}}=(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be locally optimal tokens according to Definition
2
. Let
\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}=\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}}_{\bm{\alpha}}
denote the SVM solution obtained via (
Att-SVM
) by applying the Frobenius norm and replacing
(\texttt{opt}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
with
\bm{\alpha}=(\alpha_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
. There exists a scalar
\mu=\mu({\bm{\alpha}})>0
such that for sufficiently large
\bar{R}_{\mu}
:

L1.

There is no stationary point within
\mathcal{C}_{\mu,\bar{R}_{\mu}}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
.

L2.

For all
\bm{V}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}(\bm{W}^{\textsl{mm}})
with
